Since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention during tasks?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I don't think so. I pay attention fine. I kind of have to, for my work, so... no. I don't really make careless mistakes. It's mostly just the physical restlessness I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I suppose. I don't really mean to. Just... if I overhear the junior detectives at work discussing a lead, or... even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, I tend to jump in. I'll start asking questions or giving advice before I've actually been asked. \n\nIt's usually only afterward that I realize I... barged into someone else's conversation. So... yes. I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn to speak?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Yes. \n\nIt happens with work mostly. I'll... catch myself talking through every angle of a case, or just going on and on during a client meeting. I basically fill up all the space. I'll keep going even when they're giving me clear signs they're done listening. I just... have a hard time stopping once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and tendency to interrupt or talk excessively have caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it has. It's... it's starting to cost me, professionally. \n\nI've... missed some details in investigations because of it. And I know I've irritated a few clients lately. The junior detectives are also... well, they're less willing to debrief with me now. I think they feel a bit steamrolled when I get going. So... yes, it's causing some issues.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you're feeling restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I don't think I'm focusing on anything specific when it happens. I haven't really noticed any pattern with my thoughts, anyway. It's... just what I said before. Just a physical thing.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing that you couldn't sit still?\n\nPatient: I suppose it's been a long time. Um... grade school, probably. \n\nMy report cards back then usually said I was bright but... had trouble staying in my seat, or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So... yeah. Since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention during tasks?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I don't think so. I pay attention fine. I kind of have to, for my work, so... no. I don't really make careless mistakes. It's mostly just the physical restlessness I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I suppose. I don't really mean to. Just... if I overhear the junior detectives at work discussing a lead, or... even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, I tend to jump in. I'll start asking questions or giving advice before I've actually been asked. \n\nIt's usually only afterward that I realize I... barged into someone else's conversation. So... yes. I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn to speak?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Yes. \n\nIt happens with work mostly. I'll... catch myself talking through every angle of a case, or just going on and on during a client meeting. I basically fill up all the space. I'll keep going even when they're giving me clear signs they're done listening. I just... have a hard time stopping once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and tendency to interrupt or talk excessively have caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it has. It's... it's starting to cost me, professionally. \n\nI've... missed some details in investigations because of it. And I know I've irritated a few clients lately. The junior detectives are also... well, they're less willing to debrief with me now. I think they feel a bit steamrolled when I get going. So... yes, it's causing some issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or engaging in excessive physical movement, even when you're not necessarily feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. \n\nWhen I'm stuck at the computer, combing through records and things... I guess I do move a lot. My foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I'm clicking a pen. I... shift around enough that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods in your life where these restless and fidgety behaviors went away for a while?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think they ever really go away. \n\nBut, um... it has been noticeably worse lately. For about eight months or so. Ever since I... well, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment. It's just been more pronounced since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job, or had significant trouble maintaining relationships, because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't been fired. I've always managed to keep working. \n\nAs for relationships... I don't know. I guess not. I don't really have a lot of... close ties to disrupt, anyway. So, no. Just the issues at work I already mentioned.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I pay attention fine. I kind of have to, for my work, so... no. I don't really make careless mistakes. It's mostly just the physical restlessness I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I suppose. I don't really mean to. Just... if I overhear the junior detectives at work discussing a lead, or... even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, I tend to jump in. I'll start asking questions or giving advice before I've actually been asked. \n\nIt's usually only afterward that I realize I... barged into someone else's conversation. So... yes. I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn to speak?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Yes. \n\nIt happens with work mostly. I'll... catch myself talking through every angle of a case, or just going on and on during a client meeting. I basically fill up all the space. I'll keep going even when they're giving me clear signs they're done listening. I just... have a hard time stopping once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and tendency to interrupt or talk excessively have caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it has. It's... it's starting to cost me, professionally. \n\nI've... missed some details in investigations because of it. And I know I've irritated a few clients lately. The junior detectives are also... well, they're less willing to debrief with me now. I think they feel a bit steamrolled when I get going. So... yes, it's causing some issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or engaging in excessive physical movement, even when you're not necessarily feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. \n\nWhen I'm stuck at the computer, combing through records and things... I guess I do move a lot. My foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I'm clicking a pen. I... shift around enough that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods in your life where these restless and fidgety behaviors went away for a while?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think they ever really go away. \n\nBut, um... it has been noticeably worse lately. For about eight months or so. Ever since I... well, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment. It's just been more pronounced since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job, or had significant trouble maintaining relationships, because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't been fired. I've always managed to keep working. \n\nAs for relationships... I don't know. I guess not. I don't really have a lot of... close ties to disrupt, anyway. So, no. Just the issues at work I already mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulties maintaining friendships due to your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I... well, like I just said, I don't really have a lot of close ties. I keep to myself, mostly. My work is pretty solitary. So... no. There aren't really friendships to disrupt, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Just restless, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're feeling restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I wouldn't say I'm anxious. It's not really about being worried. It's just... physical, I guess. My body just wants to move. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you're feeling restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I don't think I'm focusing on anything specific when it happens. I haven't really noticed any pattern with my thoughts, anyway. It's... just what I said before. Just a physical thing.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing that you couldn't sit still?\n\nPatient: I suppose it's been a long time. Um... grade school, probably. \n\nMy report cards back then usually said I was bright but... had trouble staying in my seat, or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So... yeah. Since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention during tasks?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I don't think so. I pay attention fine. I kind of have to, for my work, so... no. I don't really make careless mistakes. It's mostly just the physical restlessness I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I suppose. I don't really mean to. Just... if I overhear the junior detectives at work discussing a lead, or... even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, I tend to jump in. I'll start asking questions or giving advice before I've actually been asked. \n\nIt's usually only afterward that I realize I... barged into someone else's conversation. So... yes. I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, even when it's not your turn to speak?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Yes. \n\nIt happens with work mostly. I'll... catch myself talking through every angle of a case, or just going on and on during a client meeting. I basically fill up all the space. I'll keep going even when they're giving me clear signs they're done listening. I just... have a hard time stopping once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and tendency to interrupt or talk excessively have caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it has. It's... it's starting to cost me, professionally. \n\nI've... missed some details in investigations because of it. And I know I've irritated a few clients lately. The junior detectives are also... well, they're less willing to debrief with me now. I think they feel a bit steamrolled when I get going. So... yes, it's causing some issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or engaging in excessive physical movement, even when you're not necessarily feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. \n\nWhen I'm stuck at the computer, combing through records and things... I guess I do move a lot. My foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I'm clicking a pen. I... shift around enough that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods in your life where these restless and fidgety behaviors went away for a while?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think they ever really go away. \n\nBut, um... it has been noticeably worse lately. For about eight months or so. Ever since I... well, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment. It's just been more pronounced since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job, or had significant trouble maintaining relationships, because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't been fired. I've always managed to keep working. \n\nAs for relationships... I don't know. I guess not. I don't really have a lot of... close ties to disrupt, anyway. So, no. Just the issues at work I already mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulties maintaining friendships due to your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I... well, like I just said, I don't really have a lot of close ties. I keep to myself, mostly. My work is pretty solitary. So... no. There aren't really friendships to disrupt, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and impulsive behaviors are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. It's... it's starting to cost me professionally. I've missed details in a few investigations lately. And, um, I've irritated some clients. The junior detectives at the agency... they don't really want to debrief with me anymore. They say I steamroll them. So... yes. It's an issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and impulsive behaviors have affected your ability to achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: I... not really. I don't know. I mean, I've been an investigator for a long time, so I guess I've mostly done what I set out to do. I don't really have... grand ambitions that are being ruined or anything. It's just the day-to-day work issues I already told you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job or turn down a promotion because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No. I've... I've been with the same agency for years, so I haven't quit. And promotions aren't really a thing in my line of work anyway. So... no. It hasn't come to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a break from work or significantly adjust your daily routines because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I just... I keep working. I don't really take breaks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um... well, I suppose I'm here because I keep getting up when I'm supposed to just... stay put. People are starting to notice. I can't seem to just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up because you can't sit still, or because you're trying to do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly that I just... can't sit still. \n\nEven if I force myself to stay in the chair, like when I'm stuck at the computer combing through records for work... I'm still moving. My foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I'm clicking a pen. I end up shifting around so much that I lose my place in what I'm reading. So... yeah. Just restless, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or worried when you're feeling restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I wouldn't say I'm anxious. It's not really about being worried. It's just... physical, I guess. My body just wants to move. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you're feeling restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I don't think I'm focusing on anything specific when it happens. I haven't really noticed any pattern with my thoughts, anyway. It's... just what I said before. Just a physical thing.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing that you couldn't sit still?\n\nPatient: I suppose it's been a long time. Um... grade school, probably. \n\nMy report cards back then usually said I was bright but... had trouble staying in my seat, or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So... yeah. Since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention during tasks?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I don't think so. I pay attention fine. I kind of have to, for my work, so... no. I don't really make careless mistakes. It's mostly just the physical restlessness I mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I suppose. It's... it's starting to cost me, professionally. \n\nI've... missed some details in investigations because of it. And I know I've irritated a few clients lately. The junior detectives are also... well, they're less willing to debrief with me now. I think they feel a bit steamrolled when I get going. So... yes, it's causing some issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or engaging in excessive physical movement, even when you're not necessarily feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Um... yes, I suppose. \n\nWhen I'm stuck at the computer, combing through records and things... I guess I do move a lot. My foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I'm clicking a pen. I... shift around enough that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods in your life where these restless and fidgety behaviors went away for a while?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think they ever really go away. \n\nBut, um... it has been noticeably worse lately. For about eight months or so. Ever since I... well, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment. It's just been more pronounced since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job, or had significant trouble maintaining relationships, because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't been fired. I've always managed to keep working. \n\nAs for relationships... I don't know. I guess not. I don't really have a lot of... close ties to disrupt, anyway. So, no. It's... it's starting to cost me professionally. I've missed details in a few investigations lately. And, um, I've irritated some clients. The junior detectives at the agency... they don't really want to debrief with me anymore. They say I steamroll them. So... yes. It's an issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and impulsive behaviors have affected your ability to achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: I... not really. I don't know. It's just the day-to-day work issues I already told you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job or turn down a promotion because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No. I've... I've been with the same agency for years, so I haven't quit. And promotions aren't really a thing in my line of work anyway. So... no. It hasn't come to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a break from work or significantly adjust your daily routines because of your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I just... I keep working. I don't really take breaks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these restless and impulsive behaviors in settings outside of work, such as at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess. I am who I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Personal? I... I'd rather not get into my private life, honestly. \n\nBut... if you mean just dealing with people in general... I guess it is starting to cost me professionally.
